In short

₹15,29,410 was taken at Supreme HQ, Bandra in September 2026, across 213 transactions from 139 buyers. The average buyer spent ₹11,003, up 5% on the same month last year. The largest category was Memberships at ₹6,66,610. Class revenue reached ₹13,06,451 at ₹5,044 a class.

Start here

The till, and what sits behind it

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

394 people trained here in September against 305 in September 2025, and each of them cost the studio nothing extra to serve. Visits per member moved from 4.84 to 3.56; that single change is worth more than the whole year-on-year movement in the cash line.

September 2026 banked ₹15,29,410 across 213 transactions from 139 people — a movement of 2.3% against September 2025, and on the headline alone an unremarkable month. The average transaction was ₹7,180 and the average buyer ₹11,003, against ₹10,432 a year earlier.

Cash is the least interesting number in this chapter. The month delivered ₹13,06,451 of attended-class revenue against ₹15,29,410 taken, so 85% of what was collected has already been served. That ratio is what tells a studio whether it is trading or borrowing from next month.

Three findings underneath the top line: Cash was ₹15,29,410 against ₹15,64,732 a year earlier — down ₹35,322 (2.3%). Buyers was 139 against 150 a year earlier — down 11 (7.3%). 41 of 139 buyers (29%) bought more than once, and produced 34% of the month.

The base is widening

More or fewer people, and what they do once they are here

The number of people who paid to train at this studio in September 2026 was 394, against 305 in September 2025 and 355 in September 2024. The base is widening.

Breadth and depth have moved in opposite directions for two years. The studio reaches +11% more people than it did two Septembers ago, while serving them 9% less often. A club that is gaining members and losing frequency is not growing; it is churning more slowly than it is recruiting.

The loyal core halved its frequency

The same people, twelve months apart

78 members trained here in both September 2025 and September 2026. This is a true like-for-like group: the same people, the same month, twelve months apart. They made 713 visits then (9.14 each) and 461 visits now (5.91 each) — 35% fewer, worth 252 attendances.

Had the core held its year-ago frequency, September 2026 would have recorded 1,656 attendances instead of 1,404. The month is not short of customers; it is short of visits from the customers it already has.

126 of the 227 people who were here last September and not this one had made exactly one visit in that month. They were never members who left; they were visitors who did not come back. No win-back campaign reaches them, because on the books they never started.

Where the money came from

Category mix, and the two products that moved the year

The month divides as follows: Memberships ₹6,66,610 · Class Packages ₹6,10,017 · Sessions/Single Classes ₹1,84,905 · Newcomers Special ₹48,562. Memberships alone holds 44% of the cash, and the top three hold 96%. 2 of 7 cash account for half of it.

Memberships and Class Packages together are 83% of everything taken. A studio with two thirds of its cash in two products is not diversified by how many lines sit on its price list; it is diversified only if those two products fail at different times.

The commitment-length problem

The studio’s forward revenue cushion is thinning

Every membership that records a class count was bought on average 16.5 classes in 2026 against 74.3 in 2024 — a fall of 78% in what a member commits to at the moment of paying.

That is the whole forward-revenue argument in one column. ₹18,81,443 of cash this september-year band was collected against 752 memberships, each of which obliges the studio to deliver 16.5 classes and then ask again. Shorter commitment means more conversations per rupee, and the most fragile part of the year is the renewal the studio has to win every 16 classes.

The average member still holds 14.8 undelivered classes on the memberships bought this month. Those classes are not revenue yet; they are a promise, and the studio pays for them in room time whether or not the member turns up.

Pricing and the product ladder

What a class costs, and which tier wins

What a class costs depends on which product a member buys. Across the products that carry a class count, price per class runs from ₹10,264 (4 Class Package) to ₹10,264 (4 Class Package) — a 1.0-fold range for the same hour in the same room.

Where the cheap tier is also the small one, the ladder has inverted: the studio is rewarding the smallest commitment with the best price, which is the arithmetic behind a short membership book.

Trading pattern through the month

Where the cash actually arrived

The month was not even. Week by week, cash ran ₹1,38,715 / ₹5,73,138 / ₹3,91,818 / ₹3,45,650 / ₹80,089 — the strongest week took 37% of the month and the weakest 5%.

14 transactions fell on the single busiest day of the month (day 26), against 1 on the quietest. A studio that concentrates its selling into the days after a payroll date is not selling to demand; it is riding the payroll calendar, and the same shape will be read again next month as a trend.

Year to date through september: ₹1,45,27,963 against ₹1,47,84,819 at the same point last year, on 1816 transactions against 2035, from 757 buyers against 792. The month sits inside a year that is -1.7% on cash and -4.4% on buyers.

How the money arrived

Payment channel, and what each one carries

By how the sale reached the studio: pos ₹11,70,458 · checkout-pages ₹3,34,172 · mobile-app ₹24,780.

The average online ticket is ₹15,913 at its largest and ₹3,540 at its smallest. Where one channel carries the large commitments, the studio should read that channel as a product decision, not a payment preference: members do not choose a channel, they choose the least friction available to them at the moment they decide.

Who the money came from

Ten members account for more than a third of the month

Buying 139 people into ten groups by what they spent: the top tenth accounts for 42% of the month’s cash (₹6,49,400), and the bottom five tenths together for 9%.

Concentration of this size is not a risk on its own — every gym has regulars who buy more than everyone else — but it decides what a lost member costs. Losing one name from the top decile removes as much cash as losing a whole decile at the bottom, and the studio has no alert for either.

A small line that is not yet a business

Everything that is not a class or a membership came to ₹67,878 on 79 lines, across 6 products. The largest is Newcomers 2 For 1 at ₹48,562.

Read as a business line this is rounding. Read as a behaviour it is a signal: members who buy retail train here more often, and the counter is the cheapest relationship the studio has.

What the first purchase predicts

Intake quality, and the two-day window

What a member buys first predicts what they are worth. Across the Bandra intake file, lifetime value runs from ₹13,689 for a Single Class to ₹1,19,845 for a 3 Month Unlimited — a 8.8-fold range set by the first decision, before any coaching happens.

The same order appears in visits: 101.7 on a 3 Month Unlimited against 11.0 on a Single Class. The intro offer is therefore not a pricing question at all; it is the first programming decision the studio makes about a member.

Speed matters as much as product. Members back within a day of their first visit are worth ₹39,768 on average; those who take over a month are worth ₹13,918. Nothing in the studio currently owns the interval between visit one and visit two.

What else could explain it

The causes that are easy to miss, and the ones that are not

Every finding above has at least two possible causes. These are the candidates, and what each one would need to be true.

Partly

A quiet market

Cash moved -2.3% while the number of buyers moved -7.3%. A flat top line made of different parts is a market signal, not a sales-team signal.

Ruled out only if buyers and cash move together for three consecutive months.
Supported

The offer, not the price

Ticket size rose while the product mix shortened. Buyers are not being lost; they are being sold less commitment per sale.

Check next month whether average classes bought per membership moves.
Supported

Nobody owns the unattributed sale

a slice of the month has no salesperson attached.

A rule, not a reminder: every sale needs a name before it can be paid out.
Partly

Festival and calendar effects

Cash concentrated in week 1 / 2 / 3 / 4 / 5 shows the month trading to the payroll calendar. Public holidays and festival weeks move the same way every year and are frequently mistaken for performance.

Compare the same week-of-month pattern for three years before drawing a conclusion.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
FileSourceRows in fileRows for this studioDate span
Day End Report - Part 1 - New (9).csvnewmembers21,17121,171
Day End Report - Part 1 - Payroll (4).csvpayroll1,1201,120
Day End Report - Part 3 - Sessions (19).csvsessions25,67525,675
Day End Report - Part 4 - Checkins (12).csvcheckins150,821150,821
Lapsed New - Lapsed (20).csvlapsed29,14829,148
Sales - Raw - Sales (23).csvsales25,04425,044
❖ PM - Leads ❖ - ◉ Leads (31).csvleads25,31525,315
Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Cash taken. the Payment Value column of the sales export, summed on Payment Date, filtered to this studio. Refunds and voids are left in as they arrive.
  • Delivered class revenue. the Revenue column of the sessions export, on classes actually taught, with at least one attendance.
  • Buyer. a distinct Member ID with at least one payment in the month. A household paying twice counts once.
  • Repeat buyer. a Member ID with two or more distinct sales in the month, not two payments on one invoice.
  • Discount depth. discount as a share of gross value; spread is the share of lines carrying any discount at all.

The sales export carries no row for a class bought at another studio and paid here, so cross-studio spend is invisible in this chapter.

Cash is not revenue. A twelve-class package sold today is twelve classes of liability; only the sessions ledger shows what has been delivered.

September 2026 is read against the same month last year rather than a rolling twelve, because attendance and pricing at this studio both move with the calendar.

Year-on-year comparisons of September are not directly comparable across years when the festival week shifts; the pattern inside the month is shown so the shift can be seen rather than assumed.

Recommendations

14 actions for this chapter
01

Sell the second purchase while the first is being served

Front Desk

Reasoning41 of 139 buyers bought more than once and produced 34% of the cash. 71% bought once and were not offered anything further.

Expected impactA renewal conversation at the moment of highest engagement is worth more than the same conversation in an email three weeks later; on this month’s mix it is the difference between a one-purchase member and a repeat one.

02

Put every discount code under one owner and one ceiling

Marketing + Finance

Reasoning4 codes were used in September 2026, giving away ₹1,63,709 at depths between 14% and 19%. The largest single code is 37% of all discounting.

Expected impactOne owner and one depth cap turns discounting from a leak into a lever, and makes the month comparable to next month.

03

Lead with 3 Month Unlimited, not with the cheapest offer

Sales + Marketing

ReasoningLifetime value by first purchase ranges from ₹13,689 (Single Class) to ₹1,19,845 (3 Month Unlimited) across 963 recorded first-timers.

Expected impactThe first product decides the lifetime. Changing which offer the desk leads with costs nothing and moves the whole cohort.

04

Own the interval between the first visit and the second

Front Desk + Studio Manager

ReasoningMembers who came back within a day of a first visit are worth ₹39,768 across a lifetime; those who took over a month are worth ₹13,918.

Expected impactBooking the next class before the member leaves the building converts the only moment when they are certain they want to come back.

05

Put a named owner on the largest category

Sales + Studio Manager

ReasoningMemberships produced ₹6,66,610 of the month’s ₹15,29,410 across 20 transactions.

Expected impactThe largest line in any month deserves a target, a price test and a person accountable for it. Managing the total hides the composition.

06

Sell the next purchase at the point of the last one

Front Desk

ReasoningThe average buyer spent ₹11,003 and repeat buying at this studio clusters tightly around a monthly rhythm.

Expected impactA renewal offered while a member is at peak usage converts far more often than one offered after the visits have already stopped.

07

Report cash per buyer, not just cash

Finance

ReasoningRevenue per class is ₹5,044 and revenue per attendance ₹931; the buyer, not the seat, is the unit that has to be replaced.

Expected impactA studio can grow transactions while shrinking buyers. One column prevents that.

08

Staff the desk where the money already is

Front Desk + Studio Manager

ReasoningThe busiest trading hour of the month was 11:00, taking 25 transactions.

Expected impactPlacing the sales conversation in that hour costs nothing and works with the members’ own routine rather than against it.

09

Watch the discount mix, not the discount total

Marketing + Finance

Reasoning17% of sales carried a discount at 9.7% depth, against 22% at 7.7% a year earlier.

Expected impactDepth and spread move independently, and only one of them is visible in the revenue line.

10

Put a second pair of hands on the counter at the peak hour

Front Desk

Reasoning11:00 took 25 transactions, 21:00 took 3.

Expected impactThe counter is the only place where a faster response converts directly into a larger ticket. Staffing should follow the hour grid, not the shift rota.

11

Give the top two categories a bundle price

Sales + Marketing

ReasoningMemberships and Class Packages together produced ₹12,76,627, 83% of the month, across 59 transactions.

Expected impactTwo categories that are usually bought together can be priced together. It raises the average ticket without touching list prices.

12

Cap the depth and keep the spread

Finance + Management

Reasoning17% of transactions carried a discount at 13.2% average depth, on ₹25,57,453 of gross value.

Expected impactSpread is a policy decision and depth is a margin decision. Publishing an approved depth band converts an open negotiation into a rule the desk can apply.

13

Set a daily transaction target, not just a monthly one

Studio Manager

Reasoning213 transactions across the month is 7.1 a day, against 139 buyers.

Expected impactA daily number is visible while the month can still be changed. Monthly totals only ever arrive as news.

14

Separate new-member cash from renewal cash in every report

Finance + CRM

ReasoningCash per buyer was ₹11,003 this month against ₹10,432 a year earlier, on 139 buyers against 150.

Expected impactThe two lines are managed by different teams, respond to different offers and carry different costs. One number cannot steer both.

Chapter 02   Class Performance

259 classes at 42.6% utilisation, ₹5,044 a class

In short

259 classes ran at Supreme HQ, Bandra in September 2026, offering 3,293 seats and filling 1,404 of them. Utilisation came in at 42.6%, up 2% on the same month last year. Revenue per class was ₹5,044 and revenue per seat filled ₹931. 49 classes ran to two people or fewer.

Start here

The room, the booking book and what actually turned up

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

200 booked seats went empty in september. Late cancellations account for 100% of them; the rest never cancelled, and never came. The first number is a policy question and the second is not being measured at all.

259 classes ran at Supreme HQ, Bandra in September 2026. They offered 3,293 seats, 1,604 were booked and 1,404 were occupied — 42.6% of the room, and a show rate of 87.5% on the seats that were booked. ₹13,06,451 of class revenue was earned at ₹5,044 a class and ₹931 per attendance.

Utilisation is the only number here the studio cannot fix by selling harder. It is set by the timetable the month before, by the weather of the month, and by how many of the booked seats turn up. Revenue follows all three, which is why the class report and the sales report disagree so usefully when they are read one after the other.

A year ago the same month ran 273 classes and filled 41.8% of them. The studio taught -14 classes and moved utilisation +0.8 points. A smaller timetable with a falling fill means the studio is shrinking and not yet choosing where.

Nobody was lost. Nothing was converted

Where the missing attendances came from

The studio did not lose its members between September 2025 and September 2026. Of the 305 people who trained here last september, 78 came back this year and 316 were new to the month. What changed is frequency: the returning core made 713 visits last year and 461 this year, 252 fewer.

Those 252 missing attendances are 18% of this month\’s total attendance. Read as a demand problem it looks unsolvable; read as the same people coming every ten days instead of every seven, it is a scheduling and habit problem, and the studio owns both.

What the booking book says

Booked is not the same as attended

1,604 seats were booked and 200 were not occupied. 100% of those empty seats were formally released as late cancellations; the remaining 1 were booked, paid for or not, and simply did not appear. A studio that counts only the cancelled seats believes its no-show problem is a tenth the size it is.

The booking book is also where the waitlist opportunity sits. PowerCycle alone left 65 booked seats empty at a 86% show rate.

The room is bigger than the demand

Capacity, where it was put, and where it landed

The classes nobody came to

Where the timetable is asking for something that is not there

11 classes ran to nobody at all and 38 ran to one or two people. They are not scattered: PowerCycle (8 empty). Every one of those classes was scheduled, staffed and lit.

Empty classes are cheap to ignore because each one individually costs little. In a month they are the single largest block of recoverable capacity: they consume trainer hours that the filled classes could have used, and they are the first place a member looks when they want a room with nobody in it.

The month within the month

Four weeks, four different studios

Split into weeks, the month ran week 1 48% → week 2 44% → week 3 38% → week 4 42% → week 5 38%. The fade is in the last week: week 1 filled 48% and week 5 filled 38%, on 20 classes against 62.

A single-month review that ignores this shape will attribute a calendar effect to management. Weeks are also where a studio can act fastest: a class moved from week four to week one costs nothing but a text.

How the room pays for itself

Revenue per class, and what moves it

The room pays for itself unevenly. Revenue per class ranges from ₹0 (Barre 57 Express) to ₹7,252 (PowerCycle Express) — a ·-fold difference for an hour of the same floor, the same sound system and the same rent. The difference is capacity, price and who owns the visit, and all three are set by the studio, not by the member.

PowerCycle earns the most in total (₹3,92,685, 30% of the month) while Studio PowerCycle Express earns most per class. A format that earns most per class and fewest classes is a pricing opportunity; the opposite is a scheduling one.

Hosted and partner classes

Capacity that is spent on something else

3 classes in September 2026 were hosted, partner or event sessions, taking 34 of the month\’s 1,404 attendances (2%) for ₹0 of recorded revenue. They were offered 41 seats, so they consumed 1% of the studio\’s capacity.

Event classes are neither a marketing line nor a programming line as things stand, so they are counted in both and owned by neither. Put them on one page with a purpose attached — reach, goodwill or cash — and the next month can be judged on whichever was chosen.

What else could explain it

The causes that are easy to miss, and the ones that are not

Utilisation is the most over-attributed number in a studio. These are the candidate explanations for this month, in order of how much of the gap they can actually carry.

Partly

Fewer classes, or fewer people?

The timetable ran 259 classes against 273 a year earlier, so 93% of last year\’s capacity was on offer. Utilisation moves on both numerator and denominator; read the seats before blaming the demand.

If seats fell and fill held, the studio cut supply; if seats rose and fill fell, it added supply ahead of demand.
Supported

The 2-for-1 pack changes what counts as a return

A member on an introductory two-for-one is a returning client on their third visit, not their second. Read on the second visit, the repeat rate of any month looks collapsed.

Applied throughout: frequency here is visits per member from the check-in ledger, which counts what happened rather than what was paid for.
Ruled out

The weather and the calendar

A september month at this studio contains public holidays and festival weeks that move between years. The weekly split above is shown so the effect can be seen in the data instead of argued about.

Cannot be confirmed from these files: the exports carry no closed-day or weather record.
Supported

Nobody was told the room was half empty

Show rate was 87.5% on 1,604 booked seats. Published capacity, bookings and waitlist turn an unused seat into a sold one at no cost.

This is the only lever in this chapter that needs no price change.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
FileSourceRows in fileRows for this studioDate span
Day End Report - Part 1 - New (9).csvnewmembers21,17121,171
Day End Report - Part 1 - Payroll (4).csvpayroll1,1201,120
Day End Report - Part 3 - Sessions (19).csvsessions25,67525,675
Day End Report - Part 4 - Checkins (12).csvcheckins150,821150,821
Lapsed New - Lapsed (20).csvlapsed29,14829,148
Sales - Raw - Sales (23).csvsales25,04425,044
❖ PM - Leads ❖ - ◉ Leads (31).csvleads25,31525,315
Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Class. one row of the sessions export. Where a row carries a class count above one, it is read as one scheduled slot.
  • Utilisation. attended places divided by seats offered (Capacity), not by bookings.
  • Show rate. attended divided by booked.
  • Late cancellation. the LateCancelled count on the session row: a member who released the seat inside the cancellation window.
  • Empty class. a scheduled class with zero check-ins, whether or not anybody booked it.

The sessions ledger records capacity as it was configured, not as it was sold: a class capped at 12 and sold 8 has 4 seats here whether the studio meant to hold them or not.

Attendance is counted from check-ins on the door. A member who arrived and did not scan is invisible.

No revenue in this chapter is cash. It is the value of classes delivered, which is why it will not agree with the sales ledger and should not.

The year-on-year compares the same calendar month, not a rolling four-week window, so a month of differing length or a shifted festival week will show as a movement that is not one.

Recommendations

14 actions for this chapter
01

Recover the 65 booked seats PowerCycle leaves empty

Studio Manager + Programming

ReasoningPowerCycle is the largest single source of unoccupied booked seats this month: 65 of them, on a 86% show rate against a studio-wide 88%.

Expected impactThe scale of the format is what makes the number worth chasing: one class a week tighter, or a waitlist that fires the moment a seat returns, recovers roughly 16 seats a month without a single new member being asked for money.

02

Review every class that ran to nobody before it is scheduled again

Programming

Reasoning11 classes in September 2026 were taught to an empty room, concentrated in PowerCycle (8).

Expected impactEach one is a paid hour that produced nothing. A monthly rule - three empties and the slot is retired or re-timed - is the cheapest capacity the studio has.

03

Close or re-time the hour nobody uses

Programming + Studio Manager

Reasoning16:00 fills at 71% while 20:00 fills at 11% on 2 classes.

Expected impactMoving an hour of capacity from the weakest slot to the strongest is a same-month change to utilisation that needs no new members, no price change and no marketing spend.

04

Set a minimum viable class size and publish it

Programming

Reasoning38 classes ran to one or two people out of 259. A room of two costs the same as a room of ten and reads, to the member in it, as a class nobody wants.

Expected impactMerging thin classes into the adjacent slot protects the format and raises the perceived quality of both.

05

Give event classes a purpose and a number

Marketing + Studio Manager

Reasoning3 hosted or partner sessions took 34 attendances (2%) and ₹0 of revenue on 1% of the month\’s capacity.

Expected impactDecide in advance whether each event is buying reach or making money, and the next month can be judged on the one that was chosen.

06

Cut or re-time the sessions nobody comes to

Programming + Studio Manager

Reasoning49 classes ran to two people or fewer and 11 had nobody at all.

Expected impactEvery empty class costs the same as a full one. Moving those hours is a timetable edit and it is reversible after four weeks.

07

Rebuild the weakest hour of the day

Programming

Reasoning20:00 was the emptiest hour of the month at 11% utilisation across 2 classes.

Expected impactOne hour, repeated weekly, is the cheapest capacity decision available. It should be a test with a review date, not a habit.

08

Match the room to the demand, not the format

Studio Manager

Reasoning10 to 11 seats filled 62.4% while 14 or more seats filled 38.5%.

Expected impactSmall rooms fill and large ones do not. Where the timetable allows, the smaller room for the same class raises utilisation with no new demand.

09

Publish utilisation weekly, by day and hour

Studio Manager

ReasoningThe month’s utilisation was 42.6% against 41.8% a year earlier.

Expected impactA weekly number turns a monthly post-mortem into a decision the team can still act on.

10

Rebuild the weakest weekday before adding any class

Programming + Studio Manager

ReasoningWednesday filled 30.9% across 51 classes while Saturday filled 56.2%.

Expected impactShifting a class from the weakest day to the strongest costs nothing and lifts the room without touching the timetable size.

11

Test a smaller room in the emptiest hours

Studio Manager

Reasoning20:00 ran 2 classes at 11% fill, while the studio average was 42.6%.

Expected impactCapacity is only wasted relative to demand. A short class in a small room can fill without changing who books it.

12

Check the seat count against the book, class by class

Programming

Reasoning3,293 seats were offered for 1,404 attendances, so 1,889 seats went unsold.

Expected impactHalf the capacity problem is created by the room a class is put in. The seat count is editable and it costs nothing to change.

13

Decide the fate of every class that ran to nobody

Programming

Reasoning11 classes had no attendees at all against 49 with two or fewer.

Expected impactThese are individual decisions, not a category. A one-page list with a keep, move or drop box next to each one clears the question in a single meeting.

14

Publish capacity, bookings and show rate for the coming week

Studio Manager

ReasoningUtilisation for the month closed at 42.6%.

Expected impactSeats sold three days ahead is a leading measure and it is available before the class runs. Show rate at the door is a lagging one.

In short

14 trainers taught 259 classes in September 2026, drawing 1,404 attendances and ₹13,06,451 of revenue. Utilisation ranged from 29.1% to 62.7%, a 2.2-fold spread. But revenue per attendee ran only ₹783 to ₹1,329, so most of the spread is the slots rather than the trainers.

Start here

Who taught, what happened, and what the studio can actually learn from it

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

The studio\’s own payroll scorecard rates these 14 trainers on conversion (0% to 0%) and retention. Neither column correlates with who actually filled a room this month, and both are used to decide who gets the good slots.

14 trainers taught 259 classes at Supreme HQ, Bandra in September 2026, filling 1,404 of 3,293 seats and delivering ₹13,06,451 of class revenue. Utilisation ranged from 29.1% (Anmol Sharma) to 62.7% (Rohan Dahima), a spread of 2.2 times.

That spread is the number that gets managers excited and is nearly always misread. Revenue per attendee for the same trainers runs ₹783 to ₹1,329 — a spread of 1.7 times. Most of what looks like performance is the slot, the format and the room size the trainer was handed.

Two figures belong beside every utilisation number in this chapter: 11 classes ran to nobody, and 199 seats were late-cancelled against the 1,604 that were booked. Those are the two things a trainer controls that the timetable does not.

The controlled test

Same format, different hands

The same format, taught by several people, is the closest thing to a controlled test a studio runs by accident. In PowerCycle 9 trainers filled rooms between 73% and 14% — 59 points apart with the same music, the same length and the same price. Whatever is causing that is trainable.

Read the other way, the table also protects trainers: where two people in the same format are within a few points of each other, the difference in their headline utilisation is the hour they were given, not the class they teach.

The roster, name by name

Every trainer who taught a class in September 2026

The roster read one name at a time, because a table of fourteen rows is not the same as fourteen sentences, and the differences between these people are the only thing in this chapter a studio can actually act on. Each entry is measured the same way: what was scheduled, what arrived, what it earned, and what happened to the bookings on that name.

What changed in a year

The same month, twelve months apart

Between September 2025 and September 2026 the roster changed shape. 6 trainers taught fewer classes and 8 taught more; ₹5,51,755 of revenue moved to busier hands and ₹1,77,710 away from quieter ones. 0 names are new to this month since last year and 0 who taught then are not teaching now.

The largest single movement is Cauveri Vikrant at −₹86,137. A year-on-year change in one trainer\’s book is almost never only about that trainer: it is the slots, the format mix and the leave calendar read out through one name, which is exactly why this table is paired with the controlled test above.

Where the week actually goes

The roster, drawn as a grid

The week belongs to a small number of trainers. Atulan Purohit carries the most attendances (205), and Raunak Khemuka the least (17). 0 trainers work six or seven days.

A roster that puts one person on every good slot is not efficiency; it is a single point of failure and a ceiling on how full the studio can get. The hours column in the scorecard shows how narrow the good part of the day has become.

What happens to the clients

First classes, conversions and what the member does next

What happens to the people a trainer teaches. Across the studio\’s recorded intake, Raunak Khemuka turned 33% of the first classes they taught into a membership, against 11% for the lowest name with a similar volume. Those are different outcomes from the same product, the same price and the same forty-five minutes.

Convert is not the same as liked. The members a high-converting trainer leaves behind arrive with a habit already attached, which shows up later as attendance and renewal; a trainer who is liked but books nothing produces a warm memory and an empty next month. The two columns should be read together and neither should be used alone.

What the studio pays for a session

Payroll against attendance

Across 14 trainers the studio paid ₹13,06,453 for 259 sessions and 1,404 customers — ₹5,279 a class at the median and ₹1,003 per customer. Per-session pay is a flat rate; cost per attendance is not, and it ranges 783 to 1,329.

This is the sentence to put in front of any pay discussion: the studio does not pay for rooms, it pays for hours, and the margin per attendance is what decides whether an hour was worth having. A trainer who fills a 20-seat room at the same rate as one who fills 8 costs the studio two and a half times as much per customer delivered.

Whose first class becomes a member

The single highest-leverage slot in the timetable

First classes are not distributed at random. Vivaran Dhasmana taught the most of them (513); Raunak Khemuka converts them best at 33%. If the studio does not choose who teaches a first class, the timetable chooses by default, and it chooses whoever is rostered at 07:00.

The arithmetic is worth putting on one line: moving every first class to the trainers in the top conversion quartile, holding volume constant, is worth roughly 3 percentage points of conversion on 4,203 first classes a year, before any marketing spend changes.

What else could explain it

Reading a trainer table honestly

Every conclusion in this chapter is a claim about a person, so each of them has to survive an alternative explanation.

Partly

It is the slot, not the trainer

The utilisation spread is 2.2 times, but revenue per attendee spreads only 1.7 times. Most of the headline difference is capacity and hour.

Compare trainers inside one format before drawing any conclusion about them.
Supported

Volume is not quality

Vivaran Dhasmana taught the most classes (39); Raunak Khemuka earns the most per person in the room (₹1,329).

Both belong on the same page or the roster will keep optimising for the first.
Ruled out

Ratings predict attendance

Where the payroll scorecard and the ledger overlap, the conversion rating and the delivered utilisation tell separate stories.

Cannot be confirmed from these files alone: the scorecard is a cycle average, not a month.
Partly

New trainers are worse

0 trainers teaching this month were not teaching in September 2025. Their utilisation sits inside the same band as the rest.

A roster that treats new names as a risk will keep them out of the slots that make them good.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
FileSourceRows in fileRows for this studioDate span
Day End Report - Part 1 - New (9).csvnewmembers21,17121,171
Day End Report - Part 1 - Payroll (4).csvpayroll1,1201,120
Day End Report - Part 3 - Sessions (19).csvsessions25,67525,675
Day End Report - Part 4 - Checkins (12).csvcheckins150,821150,821
Lapsed New - Lapsed (20).csvlapsed29,14829,148
Sales - Raw - Sales (23).csvsales25,04425,044
❖ PM - Leads ❖ - ◉ Leads (31).csvleads25,31525,315
Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Trainer. the Trainer column of the sessions export, trimmed and matched case-insensitively to the payroll file.
  • Utilisation. check-ins against the capacity configured for that class, not against who booked it.
  • Revenue. the Revenue column on the session row: the value of classes delivered, not cash taken.
  • First class. a member\’s first recorded visit at this studio in the member file, attributed to the trainer named against that visit.
  • Payroll figures. the total paid for the cycle the file reports, with per-session and per-attendance rates derived from it.

A trainer\’s utilisation is a function of the slots they were given. Nothing in this chapter should be used as a performance rating on its own.

The payroll export is reported by cycle, not by calendar month; where it does not cover the review month the latest cycle on file is used and labelled as such.

Class revenue is the studio\’s own attribution of membership and package value, so a class full of members and a class full of single-class buyers can earn differently.

Trainers who taught fewer than four classes are shown in the tables but excluded from any comparison, because one filled room is noise.

Recommendations

14 actions for this chapter
01

Give Anmol Sharma the slots that already work

Studio Manager

ReasoningAnmol Sharma fills 29.1% of 21 classes, the lowest on the roster, at ₹1,022 of revenue per attendee.

Expected impactRe-timing rather than removing is the change: the same hands in a better hour usually closes half the gap without a single class being cut.

02

Pay for attendance, not for the size of the room

Studio Manager + Finance

ReasoningRaunak Khemuka delivers ₹1,329 of class revenue per attendee against a roster average of ₹931. Cost per attendance, not per class, is what decides whether an hour was worth having.

Expected impactA rate anchored to attendance aligns the trainer, the timetable and the P&L on the same number and ends the incentive to fill a big room badly.

03

Assign first classes deliberately, by conversion

Programming + Sales

ReasoningConversion from a first class ranges from 11% to 33% across 4,203 recorded first classes.

Expected impactThis is the cheapest conversion growth available: no new leads, no price change, only a decision about who stands in front of a newcomer.

04

Stop separating trainer assessment from the ledger

Studio Manager

ReasoningThe payroll scorecard rates trainers on conversion and retention over a cycle; this chapter measures what they actually delivered, class by class. The two currently do not inform each other.

Expected impactOne page a month that shows both, side by side, turns an opinion about a trainer into an argument with evidence.

05

Review the slots nobody attended, with the trainer in the room

Programming

Reasoning11 classes ran to nobody, and 4 trainers carry at least one of them.

Expected impactAn empty class is usually an hour rather than a person; the review should decide which one it found before anyone is reassigned.

06

Assess trainers on the slots they hold, and separately on the slots themselves

Studio Manager

ReasoningRevenue per attendee runs only ₹783 to ₹1,329, while utilisation runs 29.1% to 62.7%.

Expected impactThe two tables answer different questions. Confusing them penalises trainers for the studio’s scheduling decisions.

07

Give the strongest filler more of the week

Programming + Studio Manager

ReasoningRohan Dahima fills 62.7% across 17 classes.

Expected impactWhere a trainer repeatedly fills the room they were given, the next marginal slot should be theirs before it is anyone else’s.

08

Track who brings new members, not just who teaches

Studio Manager + CRM

ReasoningNew-member flow varies by trainer and is recorded in the check-in ledger, but it is not on any scorecard.

Expected impactA trainer who attracts first visits is doing acquisition work. That deserves to be visible.

09

Watch late-cancellation rate by trainer as a coaching number

Studio Manager

ReasoningRates range from 3.8% to 18.3% on the same booking policy.

Expected impactA high rate usually means a slot that has drifted out of the members’ week, which is a scheduling answer, not a compliance one.

10

Pair the strongest filler with the weakest slot before cutting it

Programming + Studio Manager

ReasoningRohan Dahima fills 62.7% and Anmol Sharma fills 29.1% on the same timetable.

Expected impactTrialling the best performer in the weakest slot separates a demand problem from a coaching one, and it costs four weeks.

11

Give every regular a named trainer

Studio Manager + CRM

Reasoning14 trainers taught 259 classes for 1,404 attendances.

Expected impactThe trainer relationship is the most durable retention asset a studio has. It has to be visible in the roster to be managed.

12

Review late-cancellation rate in the coaching conversation

Studio Manager

ReasoningRates run from 3.8% to 18.3% on one policy.

Expected impactA rate that high is usually a slot that has slipped out of the members’ week. The fix is a timetable edit, not a reminder.

13

Cost the roster per attendance, not per class

Management + Finance

ReasoningRevenue per attendance was ₹931 across the month.

Expected impactPayroll per attendance is the only teaching cost that sits on the same basis as the revenue it supports.

14

Watch how many trainers share one format

Programming

ReasoningPowerCycle holds 29% of all attendances and is taught by 11 different trainers.

Expected impactToo many hands on one format flattens the standard and hides who is filling the room. The trainer list for each format belongs on the programming page.

Chapter 04   Memberships Lapsed and Churned

In short

8,707 memberships are in the Supreme HQ, Bandra ledger, 3,974 of them paid. 42% of paid memberships survive thirty days and 4% survive six months. The median membership lasts 18 days and carries ₹11,091 of recorded cash. Single Class keeps its members best at 5% still live or renewed.

Start here

Every membership sold at this studio, read from the day it was bought

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

1,953 classes sit unspent on memberships the studio has already been paid for — about ₹3,74,45,405 of value sitting in members’ hands. The people who have used a quarter or less of what they bought are the most winnable names on the file and the least contacted.

The membership ledger for Supreme HQ, Bandra holds 8,707 rows covering 3,768 people. 3,974 of them were paid for, carrying ₹4,40,74,830 of recorded money. 78% of that money belongs to memberships now marked lapsed.

This is not a report about people who left last month. It is the whole book the studio has ever sold here, read as a funnel: what a membership is worth when it is bought, how long it lasts, how much of it is used, and how it ends. The month matters only because the month is where the studio can still act.

The median paid membership at this studio lasts 18 days. Not months. And the way it ends is not a single story: 1,107 memberships recorded no attendance at all, while the rest ran down their classes and stopped. Those are different failures, they cost different things to fix, and the studio currently treats them as one number called churn.

Median life

The shape of the churn book

How a membership ends, and when

The book, by the state the system holds it in: Lapsed 7,745 · Renewal Grace Period 371 · Not Activated 352 · Active 111 · Renewed 77 · New 51.

7% of the ledger is live or in a grace window. Everything else is finished. A studio that reads only its live book sees a healthy club and a small one; the whole file says the club has met, and lost, 8,097 people.

Survival falls fastest at the start and never really stops: 79% of paid memberships are still running after Day 7s, 42% after thirty days, and 1.7% after a year. The shape says the problem is in the first month, where nothing is charged and nothing is measured.

The two ways to lose a member

One never started. One finished.
1,107 memberships recorded no attendance at all, 13% of the ledger. 314 of them were paid for. These people bought a promise and never used it; the studio has their money and their goodwill, and it is losing both quietly.

The other failure is the opposite: a member who came, used the product, and finished it. Those memberships are not churn in any meaningful sense — they are short products selling short. The distinction decides whether the fix is an activation programme or a pricing change, and today it is not made anywhere in the reporting.

Usage tells the difference cleanly: nothing used 3,229 · up to a quarter 88 · a quarter to a half 86 · a half to three quarters 136 · three quarters to all 426 · everything or more 9. The members who used nothing have a median membership of 14 days; those who used everything ran 57 days. One group left; the other finished.

Did they use what they paid for?

The classes the studio owes and nobody is claiming

802 paid memberships have a usage figure recorded. Of them, 145 used a quarter or less of what they bought, leaving 1,953 classes undelivered, and 332 used everything.

Undelivered classes are the studio’s cheapest retention tool and its largest unmanaged liability. They are worth real money to the member, they cost nothing to grant as visits, and every one of them represents a member who has already decided to come and has not come.

The decline that comes first

What the visit pattern says weeks before the status changes

Churn shows up in the visit pattern long before it shows up in the status. Comparing each membership’s first half against its second, across 1,734 memberships with more than three visits to split: 63% came less often in the second half, 37% by at least half, and only 26% came more often. The median run fell from 6 visits to 4.

That is the leading indicator the studio is missing. Attendance decline is visible weeks before a membership lapses, it is computable from data the studio already exports, and it identifies people who can still be reached — unlike lapsed members, whom the file says are gone.

No-shows behave the same way: never missed runs 14 days at 71% usage · one or two runs · days at ·% usage · three or more runs · days at ·% usage. Missing a class is not a booking error; in this file it is a prediction.

Product by product

Which membership churns, and which merely ends

Product by product, the churn is not evenly spread: 1 Month Unlimited Membership ₹91,97,174 · 3 Month Unlimited Membership ₹68,58,567 · 12 Class Package ₹55,38,645 · Annual Unlimited Membership ₹43,93,149 · 6 Month Unlimited Membership ₹35,21,086.

The largest seller is 1 Month Unlimited Membership (575 sold, ₹91,97,174), which runs 34 days at a mean usage of ·%, and is still live on 10% of them. The products that hold best are not the ones that sell most, and that gap is the studio’s product question: the file is being steered by a number nobody chose on purpose.

39% of the money in these products sits on memberships that are live fewer than one time in ten. That is the pool the studio re-sells to every month, at full acquisition cost, and it is a product decision rather than a marketing one.

The month inside the ledger

What was sold, and what it is already worth

118 paid memberships were bought at this studio in September 2026, for ₹13,47,053, at an average of ₹11,416 and a median granted length of 14 days.

71 memberships that were sold earlier ended during September 2026. Their average paid value was ₹6,472 and their average usage was 66%. That is the churn the studio will read next month as a bad sales month, when it is the tail of a good one three months ago.

Of everything sold in September 2026, 3% has already been fully used, and 14% has not produced a single attendance. The sale is the least informative moment in a membership’s life.

Who is winnable

Recency, unspent value, and the ones who asked to pause

By recency rather than by value, the winnable book looks like this: attended in the last 30 days 219 people holding ₹72,29,616 · 31 to 60 days ago 137 people holding ₹16,13,338 · 61 to 90 days ago 126 people holding ₹11,88,932 · 91 to 180 days ago 299 people holding ₹40,33,490 · over 180 days ago 1,383 people holding ₹2,33,80,029.

356 people have been in the building within the last sixty days and still hold ₹88,42,955 of undelivered value. They are not a win-back campaign; they are an existing member with an unspent credit, and the difference in contact cost between the two is the entire difference in outcome.

A separate signal the studio is not using: 821 memberships were frozen at some point (9.4% of the ledger), and those are live more often than the ones that were not — 13.2% against 5.7%. A member who asks to pause is not leaving; they are the one who intends to come back and wants the clock stopped.

What the lost book cost

Churn, priced

The lost book is not an abstraction. ₹3,44,72,653 of recorded membership money has been paid for and finished — 78% of everything the studio has ever booked on this ledger. With an average ticket of ₹11,091 and a median life of 18 days, that ticket has to be re-earned every 18 days per member.

Put beside what a sale costs to win — the studio collected ₹11,003 per buyer in september — churn stops being a marketing problem and becomes the reason the marketing budget exists.

Discounting at the point of sale does not buy durability here: none is live 6% of the time. Discount and churn are close to independent in this file, which is its own finding — the studio can stop buying retention with price and start buying it with programming.

What else could explain it

Causes the studio has not considered

Churn is the chapter where the obvious explanation is usually wrong, so each candidate is stated with what would have to be true.

Supported

The product is short, not the member disloyal

The median paid membership runs 18 days and the modal product is a one-month or small-pack purchase. A short product that is fully used is not churn.

Split the churn number by whether usage exceeded 90% before deciding what to fix.
Supported

Nobody is onboarded

1,107 memberships never produced a single attendance, and 314 of those were paid for.

An activation contact in the first fourteen days is the whole intervention.
Partly

Price and discount

Discount taken at purchase shows no consistent relationship with how long a membership lives in this file.

Do not conclude discounts cause churn; conclude they do not prevent it, which is a different decision.
Ruled out

The competition took them

The exports contain no competitor or cancellation-reason field. Anything said about why members went to another studio is a hypothesis, not a finding.

Ask directly, on the day the status changes, and record the answer in the same file.

The underlying tables

Everything the chapter claims, at row level

Each block below opens onto the rows the figures above were computed from, in the same order and with the same rounding. Nothing in this chapter is derived from anything but these tables.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
FileSourceRows in fileRows for this studioDate span
Day End Report - Part 1 - New (9).csvnewmembers21,17121,171
Day End Report - Part 1 - Payroll (4).csvpayroll1,1201,120
Day End Report - Part 3 - Sessions (19).csvsessions25,67525,675
Day End Report - Part 4 - Checkins (12).csvcheckins150,821150,821
Lapsed New - Lapsed (20).csvlapsed29,14829,148
Sales - Raw - Sales (23).csvsales25,04425,044
❖ PM - Leads ❖ - ◉ Leads (31).csvleads25,31525,315
Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Membership. one row of the lapsed and new-members export, which lists every membership the studio has ever created for this location, whatever its current state.
  • Paid. a membership whose amount paid is above zero and whose name is not a staff, complimentary, credit or referral product.
  • Lapsed. the Derived Status column as the system computes it, cross-checked against the churn and end dates on the row.
  • Lifetime. end date minus purchase date, or, for memberships still open, today minus purchase date. Open memberships are therefore treated as censored, not as survivors.
  • Usage. the Sessions Used % column, which the studio records against the classes granted, not the money paid.

The ledger starts wherever the export starts. Memberships that were created and deleted before the file’s first day are not in it, so very early churn may be understated.

No cancellation reason is recorded anywhere in these files. The reports say what happened to a membership, never why the member says it happened.

Money shown is what the studio recorded as paid for the membership, not revenue recognised; unused classes are still a liability rather than income.

A member may hold several memberships at once. Counts of memberships and counts of people are deliberately kept apart in every table.

Recommendations

14 actions for this chapter
01

Write the unspent classes back as visits, with a date on them

Front Desk + Finance

Reasoning145 memberships used a quarter or less of what they bought, leaving 1,953 classes undelivered worth about ₹3,65,59,379.

Expected impactConverting credit into booked attendance moves money the studio already holds onto the balance sheet and pulls the member back into a routine - the single cheapest retention action available.

02

Segment win-back by recency, not by lifetime value

CRM + Marketing

Reasoning219 people were here in the last thirty days and still hold ₹72,29,616; the largest group, 1,383 people, has not been inside a year.

Expected impactContact cost per recovered member falls by an order of magnitude between those two groups, and the studio currently sends the same message to both.

03

Freeze, do not lose: treat a pause request as a save

Front Desk

Reasoning821 memberships were frozen at some point and 13.2% are still live, against 5.7% of those that were never frozen.

Expected impactA member who asks to pause is planning to return. Offering the pause before they offer the cancellation converts a churn event into a deferred one.

04

Put every member on a plan at the halfway point of their membership

Studio Manager

Reasoning63% of measurable memberships attended less in their second half than their first, and 37% attended at least half as often. The change is visible before the status moves.

Expected impactA mid-membership review - attendance so far, what is left, one specific class to book - is the only intervention in this chapter that can act before the lapse rather than after it.

05

Run an activation sequence through the first fortnight

Front Desk + CRM

Reasoning44% of paid memberships end within fourteen days and 58% within thirty.

Expected impactThe first two weeks decide the membership. A dated plan, a booked class and a call on day ten is the whole intervention.

06

Collect the unused classes

CRM + Front Desk

Reasoning145 memberships used a quarter or less of what they bought, leaving 1,953 classes unspent.

Expected impactPaid-for and undelivered is a liability and a warm reason to make contact.

07

Lead with the product that keeps its members

Sales + Marketing

ReasoningSingle Class is still live or renewed 5% of the time, the best rate in the range.

Expected impactRenewal is a property of the product list before it is a property of the customer. What works should be sold first.

08

Put the churn number in the acquisition plan

Management + Finance

ReasoningMedian membership life is 18 days.

Expected impactIf most of the book has to be replaced each month, acquisition is a maintenance cost rather than a growth line, and it should be budgeted as one.

09

Make the mid-length products the default recommendation

Sales + Marketing

ReasoningMemberships of 31 to 60 days carry ₹15,306 of average cash against ₹1,907 for a week-long product.

Expected impactThe short products are the ones that leave. What a member buys first predicts how long they stay far better than how much they liked the trial.

10

Convert the single-class buyers before the week is out

Front Desk + CRM

Reasoning1,144 memberships lasted a week or less and used 0.50 classes each.

Expected impactThis is the largest, cheapest and most time-sensitive group in the ledger. A call on day three is worth more than any win-back offer in month three.

11

Work the not-activated and grace-period files monthly

CRM + Front Desk

Reasoning352 memberships never activated and 371 sit inside the grace window.

Expected impactBoth are dated, small and contactable. A monthly standing list keeps this number from building up into a churn statistic.

12

Offer the unspent classes back as visits, not as credit

CRM

Reasoning145 memberships used a quarter or less of what they paid for, leaving 1,953 classes unspent.

Expected impactUndelivered classes are already paid for. Converting them into visits rebuilds the habit; converting them into credit only postpones the decision to leave.

13

Price a three-month product deliberately

Sales + Management

ReasoningThe median membership life is 18 days.

Expected impactIf the median life is inside the second month, a three-month product sets a different expectation and gives the studio two renewal conversations instead of one.

14

Read churn by joining month, not only by product

CRM

Reasoning8,707 memberships sit in the ledger, 3,974 of them paid.

Expected impactMembers who joined in the same month behave similarly. Cohort lines show whether retention is improving or whether the mix is merely changing.

In short

12 formats ran 259 classes in September 2026 for ₹13,06,451. Hosted Class filled 85.7% of its seats against 0.0% for Trainer's Choice. PowerCycle reached the most members at 181.

Start here

Every format, on more than one measure

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

PowerCycle takes 35% of the timetable and fills 35% of it. The format that fills best takes 1%. The studio’s programming decisions and its demand are pointing in different directions.

12 formats ran 259 classes in September 2026. The fullest, Hosted Class, filled 85.7% of its seats; the emptiest, Trainer's Choice, 0.0%. Revenue per class ranges from ₹0 to ₹7,252.

A format is not a class type. It is a product with its own capacity, price, room, hour, trainer pool and member base, and the studio sets those five things for each of them independently while judging them all on one number, attendance. This chapter separates the five.

The league table

Twelve numbers per format, and no averaging

The league table, in full. Sessions, seats, attendance, utilisation, revenue and the two efficiency measures - what a class earns and what a body in the room earns. Nothing else in the review will contradict this table, so it is the one to argue with.

PowerCycle alone holds 29% of the attendances, and the top three hold 73%. 2 of 12 attendances account for half of it. Concentration at this level means the studio has, in effect, two products and a portfolio of experiments, and the experiments are consuming the same scarce resource as the products: the good hour.

Reach against regularity

Two different jobs, one timetable

Reach and regularity are different properties and formats have them in different proportions. PowerCycle reached 181 members at 2.27 visits each; FIT reached 104 members at 2.66 visits each.

A format with high reach and low frequency is an acquisition and sampling product; a format with low reach and high frequency is a habit. Scheduling them as though they had the same purpose is why one of them is under capacity and the other has a waiting list.

The multi-format multiplier

Variety as a retention mechanism

38% of the members who trained here in september used more than one format, and 94 made three or more visits to a single format. The two behaviours are not in conflict: variety keeps a member coming, and a favourite keeps them accountable.

The studio currently manages neither. Nothing in the booking flow uses a member’s own favourite format to hold their slot, and nothing offers the second format to the person who has been coming to the first for months. The cross-sell exists in the membership; it has never been scheduled.

What a devotee is worth

Faithfulness measured, not assumed

Of the 394 members who attended this month, 283 gave at least 60% of their visits to a single format and 21 spread themselves so widely that no format held a third of them. The faithful average 2.10 visits a month; the generalists 8.90.

Frequency, not preference, is what the studio is buying: on this month’s numbers the devoted visit 0.2 times as often as the unattached. The commercial question is whether a generalist is a devotee who has not found their room yet, or a member about to stop.

Evidence — the format mix of the most devoted members

Devotion is the largest single-format share of that member’s visits in the month.

When each format is used

The grid, and the space inside it

Each format lives in a part of the week. The busiest day for PowerCycle is a Saturday (94 attendances), and 44 format-day combinations were never used at all.

That last number is the whole opportunity. A format that never runs on a Tuesday is not a format that cannot run on a Tuesday; it is a format nobody has tried. The white space in a grid like this is where a studio finds its next full room without touching price.

Where the free seats sit

The subsidy nobody authorised

Complimentary and non-paid seats are allocated by habit, and they land unevenly: PowerCycle 8% · FIT 1% · Barre 57 1% · Mat 57 1% · Cardio Barre 0% · Back Body Blaze 0% · PowerCycle Express 3% · Recovery 0% · Cardio Barre Express 0% · Mat 57 Express 10%.

The formats that carry the free seats are subsidising the formats that do not, and nobody has decided that. Set the budget by format against a stated purpose — trial volume, a new launch, a partner deal — and the emptiest room in the building becomes an asset with a price on it.

What else could explain it

Why the league table can be read the wrong way

A format comparison is a comparison of five different decisions at once. Here is which of them the table can and cannot attribute.

Supported

Capacity, not popularity

Utilisation is seats, and seats are chosen by the studio: the largest room carries PowerCycle. A format filling 35% of a 30-seat room is outperforming one filling 60% of a 12-seat room on revenue per class, and does not look like it.

Judge formats on revenue per class and revenue per attendance alongside fill.
Partly

The trainer, not the format

PowerCycle is taught by 11 different trainers this month. Where one format has many hands, its average is a mix of standards.

The instructor chapter holds the within-format comparison.
Ruled out

The hour is neutral

Formats occupy different hours almost by definition, and hour is the strongest single predictor of fill in this data set. Any format comparison that does not hold the hour constant is measuring the clock.

Read the when-each-format-is-used grid before reading the league table.
Supported

A new format looks like a failing one

2 formats ran three classes or fewer this month.

A format needs a dozen classes and two trainers before its fill means anything.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
FileSourceRows in fileRows for this studioDate span
Day End Report - Part 1 - New (9).csvnewmembers21,17121,171
Day End Report - Part 1 - Payroll (4).csvpayroll1,1201,120
Day End Report - Part 3 - Sessions (19).csvsessions25,67525,675
Day End Report - Part 4 - Checkins (12).csvcheckins150,821150,821
Lapsed New - Lapsed (20).csvlapsed29,14829,148

❖ PM - Leads ❖ - ◉ Leads (31).csvleads25,31525,315
Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Format. the Class column of the sessions export, with the Studio prefix removed so that two spellings of one format are one format.
  • Utilisation. attended places over seats offered, for that format, in the month.
  • Members reached. distinct member ids with at least one check-in to that format in the month, from the check-in ledger.
  • Devotion. a member’s largest single-format share of their own visits that month.
  • Free seat. an attendance recorded against an introductory offer, a comp or no payment record.

Formats are not comparable on price: a 45-minute strength class and a 60-minute cycle class are not the same product even when the room fills identically.

A format introduced or retired inside the month will appear with a handful of classes and an extreme fill. Every table here carries the class count so those rows can be seen for what they are.

Where the check-in ledger is used, it counts who walked in; the sessions ledger counts who was booked. The two differ by the show rate in chapter 2.

The comparison is within the month. A format whose members renew is not visible here at all; that is chapter 4.

Recommendations

14 actions for this chapter
01

Rebuild PowerCycle around what actually fills

Programming + Founder

ReasoningPowerCycle holds 35% of the timetable and fills 35%. The best-filling format in the studio, Hosted Class, gets 1%.

Expected impactMoving ten per cent of PowerCycle’s classes into the hours and rooms that already fill is worth roughly 5,929 attendances a month at current prices.

02

Scale Hosted Class on purpose, and measure it

Programming + Marketing

ReasoningHosted Class fills 86% on 2 classes - the best in the studio and among the smallest programmes.

Expected impactA format at capacity cannot tell the studio anything about demand. Doubling its classes for a month is the only way to learn whether the fill was scarcity or quality.

03

Set the complimentary budget by format, not by goodwill

Studio Manager + Finance

ReasoningMat 57 Express runs 10% free of charge, against 0% at the other end, on a month-wide utilisation of 42.6%.

Expected impactA stated budget with a stated purpose converts an unmanaged subsidy into a marketing line the studio can switch off when the purpose is met.

04

Market the second format to the devoted, not to everyone

Marketing + Front Desk

Reasoning94 members made three or more visits to one format this month and 38% of members touched more than one format. Frequency concentrates in the members who already come.

Expected impactOne named class offered to a member in their fourth week of a single format is the highest-yield cross-sell the studio has, and it costs nothing but the message.

05

Try the empty day, once, before calling it unfillable

Programming

Reasoning42 format-and-day combinations had no class at all this month. The studio has never tested them.

Expected impactOne four-week trial per gap, with the same trainer and hour as the successful version, turns an assumption into a fact at the cost of one slot.

06

Decide what each format is for

Programming + Studio Manager

ReasoningPowerCycle carries 35% of the timetable at 35.0% utilisation, while Hosted Class fills 85.7%.

Expected impactRevenue leadership and efficiency leadership sit in different formats. The timetable should be set by the second and defended by the first.

07

Give the fullest format more of the week

Programming

ReasoningHosted Class runs 2 classes and fills 85.7% of them.

Expected impactScaling what already fills is the least risky capacity decision available.

08

Sell the second format deliberately

Marketing + Front Desk

Reasoning38% of members used more than one format this month and 94 made three or more visits to a single one.

Expected impactA member with two formats and a routine is the member who is still here next year. Both halves of that can be designed for.

09

Stop judging formats on fewer than twelve sessions

Programming

ReasoningThe smallest formats ran only a handful of classes this month.

Expected impactA format cannot be assessed on a handful of appearances. Either commit to a real trial or stop reporting on it.

10

Rebalance the block that carries most of the timetable

Programming + Studio Manager

ReasoningPowerCycle holds 35% of all classes at 35.0% fill.

Expected impactA single format holding a third of the week makes the studio’s utilisation a property of that format rather than of the timetable.

11

Move express formats into the peak hours

Programming

ReasoningPowerCycle Express earns the most per class at ₹7,252 while the shortest formats run mostly off-peak.

Expected impactThe peak hour is the scarcest slot in the week and a short class is the cheapest way to use part of it.

12

Match the format to the room it is given

Studio Manager

ReasoningRooms fill from 37.3% to 62.4%, a gap driven by capacity rather than by demand.

Expected impactSeveral formats can be run in more than one room. Moving the emptier ones into the smaller room raises utilisation without new members.

13

Retire or re-launch the formats under a real trial size

Programming

Reasoning12 formats appear in the month, and the smallest ones ran only a handful of classes.

Expected impactA format judged on five classes tells you nothing. Commit to a twelve-class trial or take it off the timetable and stop reporting it.

14

Watch the show rate of every format, not only the fill

Studio Manager

ReasoningThe month closed at 42.6% utilisation across 259 classes.

Expected impactFormats that fill on paper but empty at the door behave differently from formats that convert bookings into attendance.

Chapter 06   Late Cancellations and Discounts

199 seats released late, 12.4% of the book

In short

199 seats were released late in September 2026, 12.4% of everything booked. That is down from 658 a year earlier. Attendance ran at 87.5% of the booked book, and the board is only 48.7% booked in the first place. Discount depth stands at 13.2% across 18% of sales.

Start here

What the studio gives up when a seat comes back late

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

The 199 released seats are worth about ₹1,85,174 of class revenue at this studio’s average ₹931 per attendance. Nothing recovers that money today: the seat returns, the credit stays, and the room empties.

199 of the 1,604 booked seats in September 2026 were released inside the cancellation window — 12.4% of the book. They were offered back to the studio and 199 of them stayed open.

A late cancellation is not one event, it is two. The member keeps their credit and the studio loses the seat; the seat returns to the studio too late for anybody else to book it. This chapter prices the second loss, shows who causes it and when, and then looks at the other thing the studio gives away unquantified: the discount.

A year ago the same month released 658 seats at 30.8% — so the rate has moved -18.4 points while the book itself moved -530 seats. A rate can improve because the denominator grew.
199
Seats released late
12.4%
As a share of the book
₹931
Revenue per attendance
₹1,85,174
Value of the released seats

What a released seat costs

Pricing the thing nobody prices

Priced at the studio’s own revenue per attendance, the month’s late releases are worth ₹1,85,174. That is not lost revenue in the accounting sense — the class was already sold — it is the revenue the studio could have taken on the same seat, or the cost of teaching to a half-empty room.

The correct unit is the seat, not the class. A released seat in a 22-room at 07:00 is worth a different amount to a released seat in a 10-room at 18:30, because only one of them can realistically be resold. Any policy that charges for cancellations uniformly is taxing the two cases at the same rate and collecting the wrong one.

Who cancels, and when

The hour, the weekday and the hundred people

The moment matters more than the person. Late releases cluster at 09:00 (39) · 11:00 (32) · 18:00 (28) · 19:00 (27) — in the hours immediately before a morning class. That is not carelessness; it is members deciding at the last possible moment, which is exactly what a lenient window invites.

By weekday: Monday 17 · Tuesday 41 · Wednesday 23 · Thursday 43 · Friday 16 · Saturday 43 · Sunday 16. The heaviest day carries 22% of the month’s releases.

Trainers differ, but not by much: Atulan Purohit 34 · Vivaran Dhasmana 32 · Anisha Shah 21 · Mrigakshi Jaiswal 19 · Rohan Dahima 19, out of 199 released seats. The spread between the highest and lowest is 17.0 times, which is small enough that hour and format explain more than the person does.

The behaviour is concentrated: 110 people produced 199 late releases this month, and the ten most frequent account for 24% of them. 29 members cancelled three or more times.

September releases by dayMondayTuesdayWednesdayThursdayFridaySaturdaySunday
17412343164316

A policy aimed at everybody, therefore, is mostly aimed at the people who never need it. The studio can address a hundred members by name, or a thousand members by rule, and only one of those is cheap.

What cancelling actually predicts

Symptom or cause, and why it matters

The membership ledger lets cancellation behaviour be read against what happened to the membership. never cancels: 2084 memberships, median 2.3 visits and 7% still live · up to 25%: 562 memberships, median 17.0 visits and 13% still live · 26 to 50%: 861 memberships, median 16.1 visits and 9% still live · over 50%: 467 memberships, median 10.0 visits and 7% still live.

The direction is the finding: members who cancel more attend less and lapse sooner. What the data cannot say is which way the arrow points. Cancelling may be the symptom of a member who has already decided to stop, in which case charging them recovers nothing and adds a reason to go. Or it may be the cause, in which case a booking that is easier to release is a booking that is easier to abandon. The studio should pick the reading that its own front-desk experience supports and test it for a quarter.

Late cancellationsMembershipsClasses completedLate per memberStill live %
never cancels2,0842.30.06.6%
up to 25%56217.01.713.2%
26 to 50%86116.14.89.4%
over 50%46710.010.87.5%

No-shows show the same gradient and more steeply: never missed median 14 days and 71% used · one or two median · days and ·% used · three or more median · days and ·% used. A member who does not cancel at all is not a member who always comes; often it is a member who has already stopped telling anyone.

The discount book

₹1,63,709 given away, and where it went

₹1,63,709 was given away in September 2026 on 17% of transactions at an average depth of 9.7%. Four codes account for all of it: NC-2026-SHQ 61,129 · ZAP10 53,380 · 20OFFSHQ 41,800 · - 7,400.

The largest code carries 37% of the discounting. A single code with that much of the total is not a pricing policy; it is a person at a desk with a permission. Whether that is deliberate depends on something the exports cannot show: who is allowed to enter it.

Do discounts work?

What the money bought

Discounted lines: 36 at an average of ₹24,951. Undiscounted: 177 at ₹3,566. Discounted lines are the larger ones here, which is what a discount aimed at commitment looks like: the studio is buying the longer product, not the impulse buy.

Members who took a discount this month came 7.17 times on average; those who did not, 4.60 times. The discount is buying attendance here, which is a defensible reason to run it. On a membership book with a median life of 18 days, 2.57 visits is the entire distance between a renewal and a lapse.

Discount takenMembershipsAverage paidMedian days liveStill live %
·8,707₹5,06214.06.4%

Cancellations and the timetable

Which hours the studio is really running

Releases are not spread evenly through the day: 20:00 loses 25.0% of its bookings while 16:00 loses 0.0%. The early hours are where a member has the most reason to change their mind, and also where a seat is hardest to refill, because the person who might take it is still asleep.

That is the argument for a waitlist rather than a penalty. In the 1 seats released at 20:00 alone, a text to three members holding no booking would cost the studio nothing and could recover ₹316 at a third fill-up rate.

What else could explain it

Four readings of the same number

A late-cancellation rate is a policy, a timetable and a set of individuals all at once. Before acting on it, the studio should say which of the three it thinks it is changing.

Partly

The policy is lenient

12.4% of booked seats came back late. A studio with a hard 12-hour window rarely exceeds 5%; a studio with no enforced window rarely stays under 15%.

Cannot be confirmed from these files: the exports record that a seat was late-cancelled, never the rule that allowed it.
Supported

It is an hour problem, not a member problem

The release rate varies more by hour than by trainer, and the peak of releases sits in the earliest classes of the day.

Re-time one class and watch both fill and release rate for four weeks.
Ruled out

A few members are abusing the system

29 of 110 cancelling members did so three or more times, and the ten most frequent produced 24% of releases.

A rule aimed at everybody is mostly aimed at the people who never need it, and it costs the goodwill of the many to police the few.
Partly

Discounting reduces commitment

Discounted lines in this month do not go on to attend more often than undiscounted ones.

Test on one product for a quarter before changing the whole ladder.

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
