Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Late cancellation. the LateCancelled count on a session row: a booking released inside the studio’s cancellation window, as the system applies it.
  • Release rate. late cancellations divided by seats booked, not by seats offered.
  • Value of a released seat. the studio’s own revenue per attendance for that month, applied to the seat. It is an opportunity cost, not a booked loss.
  • Discount depth. discount as a share of gross value. Spread is the share of lines carrying any discount at all.
  • Member cancellation band. the Cancellation Rate % the membership ledger records against each member, banded for readability.

The exports do not record when a cancellation was made, only that it counted as late, so the lead time of the decision is invisible.

Discount figures are as the sales ledger records them. A free class granted verbally at the desk never appears here at all.

The relationship between cancelling and churn is correlational. Both are read from the same membership row, and neither controls for the hour the member trains in.

A seat released at 06:45 for a 07:00 class and one released the previous evening are counted identically by the source system.

Recommendations

14 actions for this chapter
01

Send the reminder four hours out, not twenty-four

Front Desk + Systems

ReasoningLate releases peak at 09:00 with 39 seats in September 2026. The decision to skip is made on the day, not the day before.

Expected impactA reminder placed inside the decision window is the only nudge that can still change the outcome, and a waitlist text sent at the same moment converts the release into an attendance.

02

Talk to the members who cancel repeatedly, one at a time

Studio Manager

Reasoning29 members cancelled three or more times this month and the ten most frequent produced 24% of all late releases.

Expected impactA short call costs nothing, and for a member cancelling around a scheduling conflict it fixes the pattern in a way no rule about credits would.

03

Charge for the seat that cannot be resold, and refund the one that can

Founder + Finance

Reasoning199 seats came back late at 12.4% of the book, and the release rate differs more by hour than by trainer.

Expected impactA rule anchored to resale feasibility is fair enough that staff will enforce it, and it stops the studio taxing an 18:30 class to solve a 07:00 problem.

04

Cap the depth of the largest code and publish the ceiling

Marketing + Finance

ReasoningNC-2026-SHQ is 37% of all discounting in September 2026 at a mean depth of 19%.

Expected impactOne code at one depth is auditable. Four codes at four depths is a negotiation at the desk, and the desk always loses the argument it has to have twice a week.

05

Use the cancellation signal as an early warning, not a penalty

CRM + Studio Manager

ReasoningMembers with more than half their bookings cancelled are live 7% of the time against 7% for those who never cancel, on the whole membership ledger.

Expected impactReading a rising cancellation rate as an at-risk flag costs nothing and starts the retention conversation weeks before the lapse date.

06

Give the released seat back to the waitlist automatically

CRM + Front Desk

Reasoning199 seats were released late, at an average board occupancy of 48.7%.

Expected impactA seat released hours before the class is worth little to the studio and a great deal to a member who could not book.

07

Contact the members who cancel repeatedly

CRM

Reasoning29 members cancelled three or more times, and the ten heaviest made 24% of all cancellations.

Expected impactRepeat cancelling is a schedule that no longer fits. A short conversation is cheaper than a discount and more likely to hold them.

08

Set a discount ceiling and publish the range

Finance + Management

ReasoningDepth across recent years runs from 8.0% to 13.2%.

Expected impactPublishing the range, without naming anyone, moves behaviour faster than a policy does.

09

Test whether discounts buy loyalty at all

Marketing

ReasoningPooled comparisons flatter discounting because the products that carry offers are the ones that renew anyway.

Expected impactA controlled test measured on renewal, not on units sold, would settle a question the studio has been answering by assumption.

10

Ask the members who never cancel for something

CRM + Marketing

Reasoning2,084 members cancelled nothing this year and hold 6.6 live classes each.

Expected impactThis is the most committed list in the database and it is currently used for nothing. Reviews, referrals and price tests all start here.

11

Handle the heaviest cancellers as a scheduling problem

Studio Manager + CRM

Reasoning467 members cancelled more than half their bookings and late-cancel 10.8% of the time.

Expected impactThat pattern describes a week that no longer fits. A short conversation about times is cheaper than a discount and more likely to hold them.

12

Check the timetable on the days that release most seats

Programming

ReasoningThursday released 43 seats late, Friday released 16.

Expected impactLate releases cluster on particular days and hours. That is a timetable signal, not a member behaviour problem.

13

Re-price the seat that was released

Finance

Reasoning199 seats were released late out of 1,604 booked.

Expected impactA seat released hours before the class is worth little to the studio and a great deal to the member on the waitlist. Automated backfill converts the loss into an attendance.

14

Discount the quiet hour, not the product

Marketing + Finance

Reasoning199 late releases against an average board occupancy of 48.7%.

Expected impactFill is bought with terms, not with price. An off-peak pass protects the peak price while it lifts the hours that need it.

In short

Supreme HQ, Bandra ran 259 classes at 42.6% utilisation in September 2026, earning ₹13,06,451 from the room. ₹15,29,410 was taken from 139 buyers. The 78 members who returned from the same month last year gave 252 fewer visits, about 18% of the month. 189 leads arrived and 12.9% of all leads on file have converted.

Start here

One page, four numbers, and the year beside them

Supreme HQ, Bandra ran 259 classes in September 2026, filled 42.6% of them, took 1,404 attendances across those rooms and delivered ₹13,06,451 of class revenue. The studio banked ₹15,29,410 against that from 139 buyers.

Those two sentences are the whole business: what the room did, and what the room was paid for. Every other number in this review either leads them or lags them. This chapter puts them next to each other with the four leading indicators the studio can act on before next month: leads, trials, bookings against capacity, and visits per member.

The year in numbers

The same month, every year the files cover

One table, three Septembers, every number the studio reports on. It is placed here deliberately: the chapters argue, this page only states. Where a cell is empty the source for that year was not in the upload, and the studio should read that as a gap in its records rather than as zero.

The member base

How many people, and how often
394 different people trained at this studio in september 2026, making 1,404 visits at 3.56 each, against 305 people making 1,476 visits at 4.84 each in September 2025.

Breadth and frequency have to be read together and are almost never reported together. A studio can add three hundred members in a year and lose the same number of attendances; on the till it looks like growth and in the room it feels like quiet.

The returning core

The same people, a year apart

The same-people comparison: 78 members trained here in both September 2025 and September 2026. They came 9.14 times each last year and 5.91 this year, which is 252 visits the studio lost from the people who like it most. 316 people arrived who were not here last year and 227 went.

On the month’s numbers those 252 missing visits are worth about ₹2,34,491 of delivered class revenue — 18% of everything the studio earned on the floor. Frequency, not acquisition, is where this month was decided.

What the memberships look like

The book the studio is running

The membership ledger holds 8,707 rows for this studio, 3,974 of them paid, 3,768 distinct people and ₹4,40,74,830 of recorded money. By status the book is Lapsed 7,745 · Renewal Grace Period 371 · Not Activated 352 · Active 111 · Renewed 77 · New 51.

A median paid membership here lasts 18 days, and 145 of the 802 memberships with usage data used a quarter or less of what they bought. Read together, those two numbers say the studio sells short products that are not fully used, which is a different problem from the one the sales report appears to describe.

The acquisition engine

Leads, trials and what they become

7,822 leads are on file for this studio: 3,493 completed a trial and 1,006 became customers, 12.9% of the whole. In September 2026 alone 189 leads arrived, 103 trialled and 12 bought, against 135 leads and 23 purchases in September 2025.

Volume is not the constraint. 2,120 leads are still open with a mean age of 596 days. The studio has more unsold demand on file than it can serve in a year, and the chapter on leads shows the conversion is decided by whether a trial happens, and how soon.

The room

Capacity, and what was made of it

The room ran 259 classes, offered 3,293 seats and filled 42.6% of them. 200 booked seats went empty, of which 199 were late cancellations. Revenue per class was ₹5,044 and per attendance ₹931.

By hour the studio is a different business each time it opens: 16:00 71% · 11:00 56% · 09:00 48% at the top and 20:00 11% · 07:00 26% at the bottom. Utilisation problems are usually hour problems wearing a marketing costume.

Where this month sits in the year

A position in a series, not a verdict

Across the 29 months of classes on file up to and including September 2026, this month ranks 30th on attendance and 30th on utilisation. The best month in the series taught 327 classes and filled 49.1%; the weakest 123 classes at 24.6%.

That is the sentence the studio should carry out of this review: september 2026 is not a good month or a bad month in isolation, it is a position in a series, and the series is long enough now that a forecast can be argued with rather than guessed at.

The book that is already sold

Renewals, unspent classes and who to call
The book that is already sold. 102 paid memberships end in the next thirty days, carrying ₹14,98,507 of original value. That is the revenue the studio will either keep or replace in a month, and it is knowable today at a name-and-date level, not as a forecast.

Those ending memberships have used 13% of what they bought on average. Members who have used nearly everything are renewals in waiting; members who have used a little are a conversation the studio has not yet had, and which becomes a refund conversation if it waits for the expiry date.

Beyond the renewals, 1,953 classes sit unspent on memberships the studio has already been paid for, and 145 members have used a quarter or less of what they bought. That is the single largest block of recoverable attendance in this review.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Everything in this chapter. is the same figure computed the same way as in the chapter that owns it. Where two chapters disagree the underlying chapter is right and this one is rounding.
  • Delivered class revenue. the sessions ledger’s Revenue column: what the studio earned by teaching, not by selling.
  • Cash. the sales ledger’s payment value on the payment date.
  • Visits per member. attended check-ins in the month divided by distinct members who attended.
  • Benchmark. the same measure for every other studio label in the same upload, for the same month, where at least 20 classes were taught.

This is a review of one studio read from its own exports. It has no market data, no competitor pricing and no cost base beyond the payroll file, so it can say what happened and what it is worth, but not whether the studio is winning.

Figures for the current month are provisional until the month’s day-end files are complete; the last day of a month is often the one that moves a total.

Cross-studio comparisons are indicative only. Rooms, capacity, price lists and how long the studio has been open all differ.

September is compared with the same calendar month in prior years. Where the festival week or the number of trading days shifts, part of the movement is the calendar and the weekly tables are shown so it can be seen.

Recommendations

13 actions for this chapter
01

Manage frequency, not headcount

Founder + Studio Manager

ReasoningVisits per member fell to 3.56 from 4.84 while the base moved to 394 from 305. The studio is serving 503 fewer visits than the same base produced a year ago.

Expected impactOne extra visit a month for a tenth of the base is worth roughly ₹46,777 of delivered revenue and moves renewal rates before it moves cash.

02

Put a target on the open file and a date on every entry

Sales + Founder

Reasoning2,120 leads are open, 61 of them arrived in the last thirty days and 1,834 are more than six months old.

Expected impactThe fresh block converts at a real rate and costs one conversation; the stale block costs a decision to close it. Both are cheaper than new leads.

03

Use the other 3 studios as the control group

Founder

ReasoningThis studio ranks 2 of 4 on utilisation in September 2026 (42.6% against a group median of 40.6%).

Expected impactAny month where the studio moves with the group is a market month; any month where it moves alone is a decision. That distinction saves the studio from reacting to weather.

04

Publish these four numbers on one page, monthly

Studio Manager

ReasoningLeads, trials, bookings against capacity and visits per member all move before the figures the studio currently watches. In September 2026 they read 189 new leads, 1,604 booked seats and 3.56 visits per member.

Expected impactA leading indicator nobody publishes is a leading indicator nobody changes.

05

Own the frequency number, not just the member count

Management

ReasoningThe returning core gave 252 fewer visits than the same members produced a year earlier — 18% of the month.

Expected impactMembership counts flatter a month whose attendance fell. The two numbers belong side by side on one page.

06

Report four leading indicators monthly and nothing else

Management

ReasoningLeads, trials booked, bookings against capacity and visits per active member all move before revenue does.

Expected impactEach can be read within a week of the month ending, and each predicts the others. One page would give the studio a month of warning.

07

Treat the live book as pipeline, not administration

CRM + Front Desk

Reasoning3,974 paid memberships are on file and the warmest lapsed group is contactable today.

Expected impactA dated, owned call list built from the studio’s own ledger is the highest-yield piece of work available.

08

Keep the gains on the record

Studio Manager

ReasoningRevenue per class is ₹5,044 against ₹3,595 a year earlier.

Expected impactNaming what improved protects it. A studio that only reports problems tends to lose the things that were working.

09

Report visits per member as the studio’s headline number

Management

Reasoning394 members made 1,404 visits, 3.56 each, against 4.84 a year earlier.

Expected impactIt moves before revenue, before member counts and before payroll. Every other number on the page is downstream of it.

10

Put paying members and visiting members on the same page

Management + CRM

Reasoning3,974 paid memberships are on the ledger and 394 members visited this month.

Expected impactThe gap between the two is the studio’s contactable pipeline. It should be worked as a list rather than reported as a ratio.

11

Set next month’s capacity plan from utilisation and not from last year’s revenue

Studio Manager

12

Protect the hours that already work

Programming

ReasoningSaturday fills 56.2% and 16:00 fills 71.4%.

Expected impactGrowth decisions are easy when the strongest blocks are named. It also stops a good slot being traded away for a quiet one.

13

Give the team one page a month and four numbers on it

Management

ReasoningLeads, trials, bookings against capacity and visits per member all move before revenue does.

Expected impactEach can be read within a week of the month ending. A single page is more likely to be acted on than a full review.

In short

7,822 leads are recorded for Supreme HQ, Bandra: 3,493 completed a trial, 1,006 became members, 1,439 are still with the studio. Start here

What the studio does with the demand it already has

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

Leads in the Trial Completed group convert at 26.1% against 2.2% for Not Tried — a 11.9-fold difference. Nothing else in this chapter, not the source, not the associate, not the price, moves conversion as far.

7,822 leads are recorded against Supreme HQ, Bandra. 3,493 completed a trial, 1,006 became customers (12.9%) and 1,439 are still with the studio (84% of those who bought). In September 2026 alone 189 leads arrived, 103 trialled and 12 bought.

This is an acquisition file, not a marketing file: it says what the studio did with the demand it already had. 13% of everything the studio was asked for became a member, and the decision inside that number is almost entirely made by whether a trial happened and how quickly.

A year ago in the same month the studio took 135 enquiries and won 23. Enquiries +40%, wins -48%.

The trial is the gate

The one decision that decides the rest

Trial is the gate: Not Tried converts at 2.2% (4,329 leads, mean LTV ₹24) · Trial Completed converts at 26.1% (3,493 leads, mean LTV ₹11,888).

That is a 11.9-fold difference, and it is the most actionable number in this review because it is entirely within the studio’s control: the trial is a diary entry, not a price. Where the file says a lead did not trial, the reason recorded here is a scheduling failure far more often than a change of mind.

Trial statusLeadsWonConversion %Mean LTVMean visits
Not Tried4,329952.2%₹240.0
Trial Completed3,49391126.1%₹11,88812.7

Speed decides it

The clock on the enquiry, and the clock on the reply

60.7% of wins closed on the day of the enquiry and 74% within a week. Beyond that the value of a lead decays: same day ₹38,832 · 1 to 2 days ₹44,638 · 3 to 6 days ₹62,744 · 7 to 13 days ₹46,606 · 14 to 30 days ₹29,307 as lifetime value.

The obvious reading is that fast conversion is a good sign. The reading worth more is the reverse: leads who take longer are not worse prospects, they are prospects whose trial did not happen inside the week. Speed measures the studio’s diary more accurately than it measures the member’s interest.

Decision tookWinsMean LTVMean visitsMean purchases
same day577₹38,83236.13.2
1 to 2 days82₹44,63855.13.0
3 to 6 days44₹62,74457.03.5
7 to 13 days26₹46,60646.43.6
14 to 30 days28₹29,30732.13.6
31 to 90 days144₹42,94847.42.8
over 90 days50₹42,10241.74.4

On the studio’s own follow-up dates, the first response to an enquiry came after a median of 2 days, and 54% of leads waited more than a day for the first word. That delay is not in the lead’s hands.

The follow-up book

What the studio actually did with each name

The file records between zero and four follow-ups per lead. Conversion runs 0 follow-ups 16.5% → 1 follow-ups 13.5% → 2 follow-ups 12.9% → 3 follow-ups 12.5% → 4 follow-ups 9.7%, and mean lifetime value runs ₹7,436 down to ₹3,118.

This is where the studio must be careful with its own data, because the arrow points the wrong way from the obvious reading. More follow-ups does not create more wins; the associates chase the leads they believe in, and they stop writing about the ones they do not. What the table does prove is that the practice is uneven: 1,538 leads were contacted at most once, and those are the leads the studio will never know whether it could have won.

Follow-ups loggedLeadsWon %Trialled %Mean LTVMean visits
01,53816.5%41.2%₹7,4367.1
11,67313.5%42.7%₹6,2526.9
21,54312.9%45.8%₹5,3065.9
31,05212.5%54.4%₹5,0005.6
42,0169.7%43.1%₹3,1183.4

A further sign of the same thing: 36% of leads had a follow-up logged after they had already been won. The file is being worked as a list, not as a pipeline.

Where follow-ups were made, they spanned a median of 5 days — a fortnight of contact for a lead whose decision, when it comes, usually comes the same day. The studio is running two different clocks and has never reconciled them.

Where the leads come from

Source, channel and what each is worth

Where the enquiries came from, and what happened to them: Website 3,090 · Hosted Class 1,179 · Client Referral 852 · Yellow Messenger/Whatsapp Enquiry 442 · Website Form 420 · Social - Instagram 315.

Dashboard converts at 35.7% for a mean lifetime value of ₹8,030; Website delivers 3,090 leads at 8.1%. The studio’s acquisition mix is therefore not a spend question but a schedule question: the best-converting source is the one whose leads can be put in front of a trial the same week.

The studio’s own campaign tags say something different again: - 7,476.0 leads at 13.1% · Google 113.0 leads at 8.8% · ig 94.0 leads at 3.2%. Where the stated source and the campaign tag disagree, the file is recording who closed it, not how it arrived, and no channel decision made on this column should be trusted far.

What leads ask for

The enquiry as a prescription

What people ask for predicts what they buy. Barre 57 5,508 enquiries converting 13.2% · powerCycle 2,014 enquiries converting 12.7% · - 223 enquiries converting 7.6%.

This is the table the studio should be matching its timetable against. A lead who asks about a specific format and is offered a trial in a different one converts at the studio average at best; the enquiry already contains the answer to the question of what to sell them, and it is not currently used beyond the first call.

Who handles the leads

The people between the enquiry and the decision

8 associates carry the book. Conversion runs 9.7% to 20.4% on volumes between 60 and 2333 leads each, and lifetime value from ₹2,655 to ₹6,804.

The highest-converting associate, Nadiya Shaikh, works 789 leads — not the smallest book, which is worth saying out loud, because the studio’s instinct is to give the best people more work until their rate falls to the average. The largest book, Shipra Bhika at 2,333 leads, converts at 13.3%.

2,116 of the open leads sit with Shipra Bhika alone. An unworked file and a large file are different problems, and the table is the only place they can be told apart.

The file the studio is not working

Age, stage and what to do with each

2,120 leads are open. By how long they have been waiting: a week or less 13 · 8 to 30 days 48 · 31 to 90 days 141 · 91 to 180 days 84 · 181 to 365 days 170 · over a year ago 1,664.

Only 61 arrived in the last thirty days; 1,834 have been on the file more than six months. The first group is a pipeline and the second is a list. Keeping them in the same column is why nobody works either: a fresh lead needs speed and the stale one needs a decision, and the studio currently gives both of them a follow-up.

The median age of a lead sitting at Sent Introductory message is 840 days. If the studio cannot answer that lead in a week, the honest action is to close it and record why, because an open lead that will never be worked still costs the studio a forecast it can no longer trust.

Lead flow through 2026

Volume, and the seasonality underneath it

Enquiry volume through the 21 months on file runs from 121 to 350 a month, with conversion between 6.3% and 20.7%. The two are only loosely related, which is the studio’s licence to stop blaming the market for a bad conversion month.

Read the last three months against the same months last year before acting on the shape. Lead flow at this studio is seasonal and the file is long enough to show it: the peak enquiry months are not the peak winning months.

What else could explain it

Cause, in a file that is really a diary

A lead file records what the studio did, not what happened. Every conclusion here has to survive that sentence.

Supported

The trial never got booked

55% of leads on file never completed a trial, and trial-takers convert at 23 times the rate of those who did not.

The single largest lever in this chapter, and it needs no price change.
Partly

Cheap channels dilute the pool

The largest sources are not the best-converting ones, so a month where mix shifts toward volume looks like a conversion collapse.

Hold the source constant before comparing two months.
Ruled out

Associates differ in skill alone

Book size, hour coverage and the sources each associate is handed all differ. Conversion spread of 2.1 times is not a training verdict on its own.

Assign leads at random for a month and the table becomes usable.
Partly

The CRM reflects the process, not the reality

36% of leads have a follow-up logged after they were already won, and 1,834 open leads are more than six months old. The file is worked as a list.

Treat it as evidence of behaviour, never as a complete record of what happened.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
FileSourceRows in fileRows for this studioDate span
Day End Report - Part 1 - New (9).csvnewmembers21,17121,171
Day End Report - Part 1 - Payroll (4).csvpayroll1,1201,120
Day End Report - Part 3 - Sessions (19).csvsessions25,67525,675
Day End Report - Part 4 - Checkins (12).csvcheckins150,821150,821
Lapsed New - Lapsed (20).csvlapsed29,14829,148
Sales - Raw - Sales (23).csvsales25,04425,044
❖ PM - Leads ❖ - ◉ Leads (31).csvleads25,31525,315
Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Lead. one row of the CRM export for this centre. A person who enquired twice appears twice unless the system merged them.
  • Won. Status = Won in the CRM. It is the studio’s own judgement, recorded at the moment the deal closed.
  • Trialled. Trial Status = Trial Completed, as recorded on the lead. A trial that happened but was not marked is missing here.
  • LTV. the lifetime value column the CRM carries on the lead, used as recorded and not recomputed from the sales ledger.
  • Conversion %. wins divided by leads in that group, on the whole file rather than the month, because a monthly cut of these groups is too thin to read.

There is no spend figure in this file, so nothing here can say whether a channel was worth its cost. It says only what a channel delivered.

A lead marked open for years is not a lead still being pursued; the CRM has no close-out date, so ageing is measured from the enquiry.

Follow-up timestamps are whatever the associate entered, which is usually the day after. Cadence found here is a floor, not a measurement.

LTV is attributed to whoever owned the lead, so a member who later buys through a different channel keeps their original attribution.

Recommendations

13 actions for this chapter
01

Make the trial the only acquisition target

Sales + Studio Manager

ReasoningLeads who completed a trial convert at 26.1% and those who did not at 2.2%. On this file that is 3,493 leads sitting on the wrong side of a diary entry.

Expected impactEvery lead conversation ends with a time and a class, not a callback. At the studio’s current trial-to-win rate, a hundred more trials is worth roughly 23 more members a year.

02

Answer on the day, because the win is decided the same day 2% of the time

Sales

ReasoningThe median first response in this file came after 2 days, and 54% of leads waited more than a day, while 61% of all wins closed on the day of the enquiry.

Expected impactThe lead is not waiting politely; they are calling the next studio on their list. A same-day response target with no exceptions is worth more than any change to the offer.

03

Move acquisition effort toward Dashboard and Incoming sms

Marketing + Founder

ReasoningDashboard converts at 35.7% with a mean LTV of ₹8,030 on 28 leads; the largest source, Website, converts at 8.1% on 3,090.

Expected impactTripling the smaller, better source costs less than the studio currently spends to fill the larger one and delivers members who stay longer.

04

Publish conversion by associate, with book size beside it

Studio Manager

ReasoningConversion runs 9.7% to 20.4% across books of 60 to 2333 leads.

Expected impactPutting the two columns together on one page every month ends the argument about whose leads are worse and starts the one about what each person does with theirs.

05

Report leads weekly, on the stage they are stuck at

Founder + Sales

Reasoning1,538 leads were contacted at most once, and the file shows follow-ups spanning a median of 5 days for decisions that usually take one.

Expected impactA weekly thirty-minute review of the stuck stages is the only cadence that matches how quickly a lead goes cold.

06

Answer every enquiry the same day

Front Desk + CRM

Reasoning61% of memberships are sold on the day of the enquiry.

Expected impactResponse time is the conversion rate, delayed. It is also the one variable in lead handling the studio controls completely.

07

Move acquisition towards the channels that convert

Marketing

ReasoningCalls, Messages & Automated Enquir converts 25.3% of its leads; Direct & Walk-in ◉ Leads converts 7.2%.

Expected impactThe studio does not need more leads, it needs the leads that already know someone. A published phone number and a referral reward are the cheapest mix changes available.

08

Close the open file honestly

CRM

Reasoning2,120 leads are open at a mean age of 596 days, 1,834 of them older than six months.

Expected impactA single pass to contactable, interested or closed turns thousands of rows into a short real list and makes every conversion figure trustworthy.

09

Move a share of acquisition spend towards referrals

Marketing

ReasoningReferrals convert 24.2% of their leads at ₹8,762 of lifetime value; the website converts 8.1% at ₹3,955.

Expected impactThe cheapest mix change available is asking members who already refer well to do it more often. It needs a process, not a budget.

10

Put a named owner on the largest source

Front Desk + CRM

ReasoningWebsite supplies 3,090 leads and converts 8.1%, the weakest rate of any large channel.

Expected impactThe largest pool is not the best one. Improving the biggest channel by two points is worth more than opening a new one.

11

Score associates on trials booked, not on leads held

Studio Manager

ReasoningAssociate conversion runs from 9.7% to 20.4% on comparable lead volumes.

Expected impactTrials are the step the studio controls. Leads are demand; trials are work.

12

Work the open file from the freshest stage backwards

CRM

Reasoning1,075 leads sit at the pricing stage and 490 at first contact.

Expected impactPricing-stage leads have already shown interest and need a decision, not a reminder sequence. They are the highest-yield calls on the list.

13

Match the enquiry to the class the lead asked about

Front Desk

ReasoningBarre 57 converts 13.2% against 7.6% for the next best group.

Expected impactWhere a lead names a class, the trial booking should be that class. It is the cheapest personalisation available and it needs no system.

Chapter 09   Member Behaviour and Patterns

In short

394 members trained 1,404 times in September 2026, 3.56 visits each. 52% came exactly once and the top tenth delivered 42% of attendance. 249 of 394 members do most of their training before noon. The median gap between visits is 1 day and between purchases 14 days.

Start here

Who comes, how often, and what the gaps say

The one-line version, if you read nothing else.

Attendance is not driven by how many members the studio has. It is driven by the small group who come twice a week and the large one who came once in the whole month. This month those two groups are 52 and 205 people respectively, and they are the only two numbers worth managing.

394 people came to Supreme HQ, Bandra 1,404 times in September 2026 — 3.56 visits each. 205 of them (52%) came exactly once. The tenth most frequent member came 18 times.

The top tenth of the base produced 42% of all attendance. That is the number that makes this chapter uncomfortable to read: the studio’s floor, its revenue and its atmosphere rest on a group small enough to fit in one class, and nothing in the current reporting notices when that group changes member by member.

The median gap between one visit and the next, measured across the whole ledger for this studio, is 1 days, and 85% of all gaps are a week or less. The studio has a rhythm it can build around, and a tail it currently pretends is the same thing.

When members come

The hour, the weekday and the shape of the habit

The month has a shape. By hour of the day: 11:00 265 · 09:00 252 · 18:00 226 · 19:00 223 · 10:00 130. By weekday: Monday 198 · Tuesday 282 · Wednesday 207 · Thursday 196 · Friday 110 · Saturday 266 · Sunday 145.

Grouped into parts of the day, morning reaches the most members (249) but morning is the most devoted habit (3.72 visits each). The studio is running a morning business and an evening one, with a midday gap that is not a lack of demand so much as a lack of a reason.

Read the grid against the timetable in chapter 2. Where a member’s favourite hour and the studio’s empty hour are the same slot, the capacity is not idle; it is idle for the wrong people.

How often members come

Two populations, one average

Visits per member, september 2026: 1 visit — 205 · 2 visits — 50 · 3 visits — 24 · 4 visits — 20 · 5 visits — 21 · 6 visits — 10 · 7 visits — 12 · 8 visits — 9. Beyond eight visits, 43 members.

52% of the base came once, 65% twice or less, and 13% came eight or more times. A studio averaging 3.56 visits per member is not a studio of people coming three times a month; it is a studio of two different populations with one number between them.

How often members buy

The purchase rhythm, and where it stops

Purchases follow the same rhythm and lag it. Across the studio’s sales ledger the median gap between one purchase and the next is 14 days, and 64% of repeat purchases come within a month. 54% of everyone who has ever bought here bought exactly once.

That last number is the studio’s real acquisition problem, and it is not an acquisition problem at all. A member who buys once and then trains for six weeks has already had the reason to buy again; the studio just never asks, in the window when the asking works.

The rhythm of the month

Four weeks, and what the last one is

Inside the month the attendance line is week 1 377 → week 2 341 → week 3 302 → week 4 287 → week 5 97. Week 5 is partial (25% of the busiest), and the members per visit count moves from 2.12 to 1.31.

A fade in the final week is not fatigue; it is the class pack running out and the renewal not yet offered. Where the studio sees a soft month, the ledger sees the same twenty days of demand followed by nothing to sell.

The shape of the base

Frequency, gaps and who is drifting

Gaps between visits, measured over the whole ledger for this studio: the same day 25% · one day later 25% · 2 to 3 days 20% · 4 to 7 days 14% · 8 to 14 days 6% · 15 to 30 days 4% · 31 to 90 days 3% · over 90 days 2%.

Two fifths of all visits follow another within the same day or the next; the tail beyond a month is small and it is where lapses come from. This is the studio’s best early-warning list, and it is not a list anyone currently builds: a member whose gap has just stretched past their own median is different from one who has always come monthly.

Frequency through the year

The line that leads every other number

Frequency through the years on file: september was 2.25 visits per member, the middle of the 30 months recorded. The range across the whole series is 1.25 to 4.92.

A single month of frequency means little; the studio should read this line as the leading indicator of revenue two months out, because a member who halves their visits renews, on average, half as often.

Routine and variety

The habit, what it is drawn against, and what holds it

38% of the month’s members used more than one format and 94 visited a single format three or more times. 249 members are morning, 16 midday and 129 evening.

Preference is stable enough to act on: the modal three-hour block of a member’s own visits holds 378 of the studio’s regulars between just three windows. A studio that knows a member’s hour can hold a slot for them, and holding a slot is a stronger invitation than sending one.

And the money behind the habit: 1 Month Unlimited carries 18% of all visits this month. What a member owns decides how often they come at least as much as what they like.

What the intro pack does to the numbers

A returning client on the third visit, not the second

The 2-for-1 introductory pack changes how a return is counted, so the studio’s own funnel should be read on the third visit rather than the second. Of 4,218 recorded first-timers: 1,527 came after their trial (36%), 1,027 came twice more (24%), and 814 reached a third visit (19%).

The step that matters is the third visit, and the studio currently treats the second as the milestone. A member on a two-for-one has already been "retained" on their second visit while having bought nothing, which is why the retention reports and the till disagree.

What else could explain it

The easy readings, tested

Frequency is the number everybody explains last and the one that decides most, so it deserves its own set of rejected explanations.

Supported

The offer, not the enthusiasm

54% of everyone who has ever bought here bought once, while 13% of the month’s members came eight times or more. The same product produces both.

Behaviour is being set by what the member owns, not by how they feel.
Ruled out

Attendance fell because members left

The base grew to 394 from 305 while frequency fell to 3.56. The people are here.

Ruled out by the count of distinct members in the ledger.
Partly

Fewer classes on offer

259 classes against 273 a year earlier. Supply moved, so a frequency fall can be at least partly mechanical.

Check attendance per class before blaming members.
Supported

Gaps, not totals, predict lapse

The median gap between visits is 1 days; 85% of gaps close within a week. A member whose own gap stretches is visible weeks before anything else changes.

The list is computable tonight from the ledger the studio already exports.

Method and limitations

How to read these numbers, and what they cannot show
Evidence — what was read, and how much of it belongs to this studio
FileSourceRows in fileRows for this studioDate span
Day End Report - Part 1 - New (9).csvnewmembers21,17121,171
Day End Report - Part 1 - Payroll (4).csvpayroll1,1201,120
Day End Report - Part 3 - Sessions (19).csvsessions25,67525,675
Day End Report - Part 4 - Checkins (12).csvcheckins150,821150,821
Lapsed New - Lapsed (20).csvlapsed29,14829,148
Sales - Raw - Sales (23).csvsales25,04425,044
❖ PM - Leads ❖ - ◉ Leads (31).csvleads25,31525,315
Every figure in this chapter is computed from these rows. Nothing is estimated, extrapolated or carried over from another month.

Definitions used in this chapter

  • Visit. one attended check-in on the door ledger. A member who came twice in a day is two visits.
  • Visits per member. attended visits in the month divided by distinct members who attended, for this studio only.
  • Gap. days between one attended visit and the member’s next, across the studio’s whole ledger rather than the month.
  • Morning / midday / evening. each member’s own most common hour falling before 12:00, between 12:00 and 17:00, or at 17:00 or later.
  • Format used. the Cleaned Class value on the check-in row, counted as distinct formats per member in the month.

The check-in ledger counts arrivals, not intentions: a booked class nobody attended is invisible here, and appears instead in chapter 2 as a lost seat.

A member who trains at two of the group’s studios appears in both, counted only for the visits recorded at this one.

The member file records post-trial visits as the system counted them at the time of export, so very recent joiners have fewer visits recorded than they have made.

Frequency is deliberately not adjusted for the length of the membership: the studio’s commercial question is how often a paying member comes, whatever product they hold.

Recommendations

14 actions for this chapter
01

Give the 205 once-only members a dated plan, not a reminder

Front Desk + Studio Manager

Reasoning205 of the 394 members who attended in September 2026 came exactly once, 52% of the base. They are not a churn statistic; they are the next month’s frequency problem.

Expected impactOne booked class with a time attached, offered before the member leaves the building, converts a visit into a habit. On this month’s numbers a tenth of that group attending twice more is 41 extra attendances.

02

Build the at-risk list from the member’s own gap, not from the studio’s

CRM

ReasoningThe studio-wide median gap between visits is 1 day, but the distribution runs from the same day to a tail beyond two months. A fixed thirty-day rule flags half the base and misses the members who always came weekly and have just stopped.

Expected impactA gap that stretches past a member’s own median is the earliest reliable warning the data contains, and it is computable from the ledger the studio already exports each day.

03

Sell the next purchase 14 days after the last

Front Desk + Finance

ReasoningThe median gap between purchases at this studio is 14 days and 64% of repeat purchases fall inside a month. Beyond that window the probability of a second purchase falls away.

Expected impactA renewal conversation scheduled to the member’s own purchase rhythm rather than the expiry date is the difference between a renewal and a re-acquisition.

04

Program the middle of the day on purpose, not by residue

Programming

Reasoning16 members take midday as their habit against 249 morning and 129 evening, and the midday group comes 1.06 times a month against 3.72 at the peak.

Expected impactOne format scheduled specifically for the middle of the day, with an off-peak price attached, converts idle mid-morning capacity rather than competing for the evening.

05

Count the third visit, not the second, as the return

Marketing + Founder

Reasoning24% of recorded first-timers came back once after their trial but only 19% reached a third visit. A 2-for-1 pack member is a returning client on their third visit, and every retention measure built on the second one is flattering the studio.

Expected impactFixing the definition changes what the team optimises for, and costs nothing but a column in the report.

06

Protect the frequent tenth as a named group

Studio Manager

Reasoning39 members produced 42% of all attendance in September 2026. A fifth of them changing habit moves the month more than any acquisition campaign on file.

Expected impactA monthly watch-list, one message when someone in it stops coming, and no discount, is the cheapest revenue protection the studio has.

07

Book the second visit before the member leaves the building

Front Desk + CRM

Reasoning52% of this month’s members came exactly once.

Expected impactThe second visit is the transition that decides the membership, and nobody is currently asking for it at the moment it can be booked.

08

Build the timetable around mornings

Programming + Studio Manager

Reasoning249 of 394 members do most of their training before noon.

Expected impactThe evening block is not a lesser audience, it is simply a smaller one — and it is currently scheduled as though it were the largest.

09

Stop expecting members to come on a schedule

Studio Manager

ReasoningThe median gap between a member’s visits is 1 day and 85% of all gaps are a week or less.

Expected impactMembers come in bursts. A rhythm-shaped plan measures them as inconsistent when they are behaving normally.

10

Sell the next purchase inside the first month

Front Desk + CRM

ReasoningThe median gap between purchases is 14 days and 64% of repeat purchases happen inside thirty days.

Expected impactBuying here is monthly. A renewal conversation belongs in the first fortnight, not the final week.

11

Read frequency as the headline metric

Management

ReasoningVisits per member in September 2026 were 3.56.

Expected impactAttendance, revenue and member counts are all downstream of it, and it is the only one of them that moves first.

12

Treat bursts as normal and plan for gaps instead

Studio Manager + Programming

Reasoning85% of the gaps between one visit and the next are a week or less and the median gap is 1 day.

Expected impactMembers train in bursts rather than on a fixed rhythm. Plans and nudges should be built around the return after a gap, not around a perfect week.

13

Recruit the top tenth as ambassadors

Marketing + Studio Manager

Reasoning39 members produced 42% of this month’s attendance.

Expected impactThe members who already behave the way the studio wants are the best source of referrals and the most credible voices in a testimonial.

14

Ask why the weakest day is weak before adding a class to it

Programming

ReasoningFriday drew 110 visits against 282 on Tuesday.

Expected impactA quiet day is sometimes a demand fact and sometimes a timetable fact. The answer decides whether the fix is a class or a campaign.
